I guess I’m here because... well, I just can’t shake the feeling that work isn’t safe anymore. Which is weird to say out loud, because my office building is actually really secure. I don't know, it's just this constant, heavy feeling. I... I don't really know if it comes and goes. I haven't really kept track of it, honestly. It's hard to tell. I guess I just notice it the most when I first sit down at my desk in the morning. I have this really nice ergonomic chair, actually. The company bought everyone these expensive Herman Miller ones last year because HR was doing this massive "workplace wellness" initiative. Which was kind of a joke, to be honest, because they gave us fancy chairs but then expected us to work 60-hour weeks right before the big server migration. 

My coworker, Dave, actually managed to break his new chair within the first week. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... it's really obvious when I'm doing code reviews. I'll just snap at people over the smallest things. Like, if someone submits a sloppy rollback plan, or if they even slightly question my timeline, I just lose it. I hear myself sounding so harsh and making it personal, and by the time I realize I'm doing it, it's too late to pull it back. 

It happened with Dave the other day, actually. He was questioning my timeline for the database migration, and I just bit his head off. Which, to be fair, Dave is always questioning timelines because he's terrible at estimating his own work. He uses this weird Agile methodology that he read about on some obscure blog, and he insists on having these "stand-up" meetings that always take forty-five minutes instead of fifteen. We're supposed to just say what we did yesterday, what we're doing today, and if there are blockers, but Dave turns it into a whole philosophical debate about server architecture. 

And then he types notes on that loud mechanical keyboard of his. I swear, the click-clack is going to drive me insane. I even looked up if I could expense noise-canceling headphones, but the IT policy says we can only order from their approved vendor list, and the ones they offer are those awful bulky ones that pinch your ears. I have really sensitive ears, actually. I can't even wear cheap earrings because the nickel makes my earlobes swell up...

Wait, sorry. The heavy feeling. Yeah, I just... I get so irritable. I snap at people in code reviews.

No, no, not at all. That's the thing. I used to be the one who would patiently explain why a rollback plan was sloppy, you know? I'd sit down with the junior devs and walk them through it. I tried to tell Dave about it once, but he just laughed and said his communication style was "direct." Which is just code for being a jerk, if you ask me. 

He sent this email last week to the whole department about the fridge policy, and it was so aggressive. Just because someone moved his oat milk to the bottom shelf. I mean, it's a shared fridge, Dave, you don't own the middle shelf... 

Oh, right, snapping at people. Yeah, no, it's definitely new. Like I said, I just... I snap over small things, like the rollback plans or timeline questions, and I sound so harsh and personal. And I hear myself doing it, but I just can't pull it back in time. It's really not like me.

Well, I don't really know about outside of work, honestly. I've been working so much lately that I barely see my friends. We used to do this trivia night every Thursday at this pub near my apartment. It was so much fun, we had a team name and everything—"The Quizards of Waverly Place," which my friend Jessica came up with because she's obsessed with old Disney Channel shows. She actually works at Disney now, doing something in their marketing department, which sounds amazing but she says the commute is just brutal. She has to drive on the 405 every morning, and I don't know if you've ever driven on the 405 during rush hour, but it's basically a parking lot. I used to commute an hour each way at my old job, and I listened to so many true crime podcasts that I started getting paranoid about walking to my car at night. 

Anyway... what was the question? Oh, snapping at people. Yeah, I don't know about friends or family, but like I was saying, it's really bad at work. In those code reviews, I just snap over the smallest things. Like, if they have a sloppy rollback plan or if someone even questions my timeline. I hear myself getting so harsh and making it personal, and I just can't stop it before it's too late. It's just really frustrating.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean, it's not just at work, it's kind of everywhere now. Like on the train... I take the Red Line, and it's always so packed, especially since they changed the schedule. They cut back the trains by like, two an hour during off-peak times, which makes no sense because people still need to get places. The mayor promised they were going to improve public transit, but honestly, it's just gotten worse. I read this article the other day about how they're spending millions on these new digital billboards for the stations, but they can't even fix the escalators. The escalator at my stop has been broken for three weeks, and there's this guy who plays the saxophone right at the bottom of the stairs. He's actually really good, he plays a lot of Coltrane, but it echoes so loudly in the tunnel... 

Sorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling on edge. Yeah, on the train, or even just in meetings or at lunch with the team... I'm just constantly scanning everything. I automatically clock where the exits are, I'm watching to see who's standing too close to me, and I always have one ear tuned to whatever noises are happening in the hallway. I just can't seem to be fully present anywhere anymore because I'm so busy watching everything else.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... it's been a while now. It all started with that break-in we had during an overnight deployment. That was... gosh, about two years and four months ago now? It was right around the time my office got that new coffee machine for the breakroom, the one that takes those little pods. I remember because everyone was so excited about it, but then they only stocked the decaf pods for like the first three weeks. It was a total disaster, especially for an overnight deployment when you really need the caffeine. My coworker, Dave, he actually started bringing in his own French press, but then he left it in the sink and it got broken by the cleaning crew. They felt so bad they left a little note and a gift card to Starbucks, which was sweet, but Dave was still pretty annoyed because it was a vintage French press he got at a flea market...

Wait, sorry. The break-in. Yeah. It's been going on since then. About two years and four months.

He uses this weird Agile methodology that he read about on some obscure blog, and he insists on having these \"stand-up\" meetings that always take forty-five minutes instead of fifteen. We're supposed to just say what we did yesterday, what we're doing today, and if there are blockers, but Dave turns it into a whole philosophical debate about server architecture. \n\nAnd then he types notes on that loud mechanical keyboard of his. I actually really liked mentoring. When I was an intern at my first company, this senior analyst named Sarah took me under her wing, and she was just brilliant. She taught me so much about database architecture, but also about how to navigate office politics, which is honestly half the job. She had this rule where she'd never send an email when she was angry—she'd type it out in a draft, leave it overnight, and then delete it the next morning. It's such good advice. I tried to tell Dave about it once, but he just laughed and said his communication style was \"direct.\" Which is just code for being a jerk, if you ask me. \n\nHe sent this email last week to the whole department about the fridge policy, and it was so aggressive. Just because someone moved his oat milk to the bottom shelf. I mean, it's a shared fridge, Dave, you don't own the middle shelf... \n\nOh, right, snapping at people. Yeah, no, it's definitely new. Like I said, I just... I snap over small things, like the rollback plans or timeline questions, and I sound so harsh and personal. And I hear myself doing it, but I just can't pull it back in time. It's really not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting irritable outside of work, like with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know about outside of work, honestly. I've been working so much lately that I barely see my friends. We used to do this trivia night every Thursday at this pub near my apartment. It was so much fun, we had a team name and everything—\"The Quizards of Waverly Place,\" which my friend Jessica came up with because she's obsessed with old Disney Channel shows. She actually works at Disney now, doing something in their marketing department, which sounds amazing but she says the commute is just brutal. She has to drive on the 405 every morning, and I don't know if you've ever driven on the 405 during rush hour, but it's basically a parking lot. It's just really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at your family or sister like you do at work?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister... I mean, she hasn't really been around much either. She taught me so much about database architecture, but also about how to navigate office politics, which is honestly half the job. She has to drive on the 405 every morning, and I don't know if you've ever driven on the 405 during rush hour, but it's basically a parking lot. Yeah, it happens a lot. Like, if I hear a badge scanner beeping, or heavy footsteps out in the hallway, or especially if I get a late-night deployment alert... She had this rule where she'd never send an email when she was angry—she'd type it out in a draft, leave it overnight, and then delete it the next morning. It's such good advice. I tried to tell Dave about it once, but he just laughed and said his communication style was \"direct.\" Which is just code for being a jerk, if you ask me. \n\nHe sent this email last week to the whole department about the fridge policy, and it was so aggressive. Just because someone moved his oat milk to the bottom shelf. I mean, it's a shared fridge, Dave, you don't own the middle shelf... \n\nOh, right, snapping at people. Yeah, no, it's definitely new. Like I said, I just... I snap over small things, like the rollback plans or timeline questions, and I sound so harsh and personal. And I hear myself doing it, but I just can't pull it back in time. It's really not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting irritable outside of work, like with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know about outside of work, honestly. I've been working so much lately that I barely see my friends. We used to do this trivia night every Thursday at this pub near my apartment. It was so much fun, we had a team name and everything—\"The Quizards of Waverly Place,\" which my friend Jessica came up with because she's obsessed with old Disney Channel shows. She actually works at Disney now, doing something in their marketing department, which sounds amazing but she says the commute is just brutal. She has to drive on the 405 every morning, and I don't know if you've ever driven on the 405 during rush hour, but it's basically a parking lot. It's just really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at your family or sister like you do at work?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister... I mean, she hasn't really been around much either. She just moved in with her boyfriend, Mark, and they're in that honeymoon phase where they do everything together. They just bought this espresso machine for their new place, one of those really fancy Italian ones that takes up half the counter. Mark is super into coffee roasting now, which is fine, I guess, but he talks about bean origins for like twenty minutes before you can even get a cup. I tried to tell him I just like standard dark roast, but he looked at me like I insulted his child. I don't even drink that much coffee anyway, it makes me jittery. I switched to green tea a few months ago because I read it's better for your metabolism, though honestly, I haven't noticed a difference. I'll start talking, and I can hear how harsh and personal I'm being, but I just can't stop myself from saying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's not just at work, it's kind of everywhere now. Like on the train... I take the Red Line, and it's always so packed, especially since they changed the schedule. They cut back the trains by like, two an hour during off-peak times, which makes no sense because people still need to get places. The mayor promised they were going to improve public transit, but honestly, it's just gotten worse. I read this article the other day about how they're spending millions on these new digital billboards for the stations, but they can't even fix the escalators. The escalator at my stop has been broken for three weeks, and there's this guy who plays the saxophone right at the bottom of the stairs. He's actually really good, he plays a lot of Coltrane, but it echoes so loudly in the tunnel... \n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling on edge. Yeah, on the train, or even just in meetings or at lunch with the team... I'm just constantly scanning everything. I just can't seem to be fully present anywhere anymore because I'm so busy watching everything else.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened recently that you think might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's been a while now. It all started with that break-in we had during an overnight deployment. That was... gosh, about two years and four months ago now? It was right around the time my office got that new coffee machine for the breakroom, the one that takes those little pods. I remember because everyone was so excited about it, but then they only stocked the decaf pods for like the first three weeks. My coworker, Dave, he actually started bringing in his own French press, but then he left it in the sink and it got broken by the cleaning crew. I just... I don't know if it stops. I just know it's there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that happens when you feel this heavy feeling, like when you're working on a project or talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... it's really obvious when I'm doing code reviews. I'll just snap at people over the smallest things. Like, if someone submits a sloppy rollback plan, or if they even slightly question my timeline, I just lose it. I hear myself sounding so harsh and making it personal, and by the time I realize I'm doing it, it's too late to pull it back. \n\nIt happened with Dave the other day, actually. He was questioning my timeline for the database migration, and I just bit his head off. Which, to be fair, Dave is always questioning timelines because he's terrible at estimating his own work. He uses this weird Agile methodology that he read about on some obscure blog, and he insists on having these \"stand-up\" meetings that always take forty-five minutes instead of fifteen. We're supposed to just say what we did yesterday, what we're doing today, and if there are blockers, but Dave turns it into a whole philosophical debate about server architecture. \n\nAnd then he types notes on that loud mechanical keyboard of his. I swear, the click-clack is going to drive me insane. I even looked up if I could expense noise-canceling headphones, but the IT policy says we can only order from their approved vendor list, and the ones they offer are those awful bulky ones that pinch your ears. I have really sensitive ears, actually. I can't even wear cheap earrings because the nickel makes my earlobes swell up...\n\nWait, sorry. The heavy feeling. Yeah, I just... I get so irritable. I snap at people in code reviews.\n\nDoctor: Was it usual for you to snap at people during code reviews before you started feeling this heavy feeling?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. That's the thing. I used to be the one who would patiently explain why a rollback plan was sloppy, you know? I'd sit down with the junior devs and walk them through it. I actually really liked mentoring. When I was an intern at my first company, this senior analyst named Sarah took me under her wing, and she was just brilliant. She taught me so much about database architecture, but also about how to navigate office politics, which is honestly half the job. She had this rule where she'd never send an email when she was angry—she'd type it out in a draft, leave it overnight, and then delete it the next morning. It's such good advice. I tried to tell Dave about it once, but he just laughed and said his communication style was \"direct.\" Which is just code for being a jerk, if you ask me. \n\nHe sent this email last week to the whole department about the fridge policy, and it was so aggressive. Just because someone moved his oat milk to the bottom shelf. I mean, it's a shared fridge, Dave, you don't own the middle shelf... \n\nOh, right, snapping at people. Yeah, no, it's definitely new. Like I said, I just... I snap over small things, like the rollback plans or timeline questions, and I sound so harsh and personal. And I hear myself doing it, but I just can't pull it back in time. It's really not like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at people or getting irritable outside of work, like with your family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't really know about outside of work, honestly. I've been working so much lately that I barely see my friends. We used to do this trivia night every Thursday at this pub near my apartment. It was so much fun, we had a team name and everything—\"The Quizards of Waverly Place,\" which my friend Jessica came up with because she's obsessed with old Disney Channel shows. She actually works at Disney now, doing something in their marketing department, which sounds amazing but she says the commute is just brutal. She has to drive on the 405 every morning, and I don't know if you've ever driven on the 405 during rush hour, but it's basically a parking lot. I used to commute an hour each way at my old job, and I listened to so many true crime podcasts that I started getting paranoid about walking to my car at night. \n\nAnyway... what was the question? Oh, snapping at people. Yeah, I don't know about friends or family, but like I was saying, it's really bad at work. In those code reviews, I just snap over the smallest things. Like, if they have a sloppy rollback plan or if someone even questions my timeline. I hear myself getting so harsh and making it personal, and I just can't stop it before it's too late. It's just really frustrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself snapping at your family or sister like you do at work?\n\nPatient: Well, my sister... I mean, she hasn't really been around much either. She just moved in with her boyfriend, Mark, and they're in that honeymoon phase where they do everything together. They just bought this espresso machine for their new place, one of those really fancy Italian ones that takes up half the counter. Mark is super into coffee roasting now, which is fine, I guess, but he talks about bean origins for like twenty minutes before you can even get a cup. I tried to tell him I just like standard dark roast, but he looked at me like I insulted his child. I don't even drink that much coffee anyway, it makes me jittery. I switched to green tea a few months ago because I read it's better for your metabolism, though honestly, I haven't noticed a difference. I get the matcha powder from this little shop downtown, but parking there is a nightmare... \n\nWait, sorry. Snapping at my sister. Honestly, I haven't seen her enough to snap at her. Like I keep saying, it's really just at work. It's those code reviews. I'll just lose it over something so small, like a bad rollback plan or if someone pushes back on my timeline. I'll start talking, and I can hear how harsh and personal I'm being, but I just can't stop myself from saying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or on edge when you're not at work, like when you're at home or out with friends?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, it's not just at work, it's kind of everywhere now. Like on the train... I take the Red Line, and it's always so packed, especially since they changed the schedule. They cut back the trains by like, two an hour during off-peak times, which makes no sense because people still need to get places. The mayor promised they were going to improve public transit, but honestly, it's just gotten worse. I read this article the other day about how they're spending millions on these new digital billboards for the stations, but they can't even fix the escalators. The escalator at my stop has been broken for three weeks, and there's this guy who plays the saxophone right at the bottom of the stairs. He's actually really good, he plays a lot of Coltrane, but it echoes so loudly in the tunnel... \n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, feeling on edge. Yeah, on the train, or even just in meetings or at lunch with the team... I'm just constantly scanning everything. I automatically clock where the exits are, I'm watching to see who's standing too close to me, and I always have one ear tuned to whatever noises are happening in the hallway. I just can't seem to be fully present anywhere anymore because I'm so busy watching everything else.\n\nDoctor: Has something specific happened recently that you think might be connected to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... it's been a while now. It all started with that break-in we had during an overnight deployment. That was... gosh, about two years and four months ago now? It was right around the time my office got that new coffee machine for the breakroom, the one that takes those little pods. I remember because everyone was so excited about it, but then they only stocked the decaf pods for like the first three weeks. It was a total disaster, especially for an overnight deployment when you really need the caffeine. My coworker, Dave, he actually started bringing in his own French press, but then he left it in the sink and it got broken by the cleaning crew. They felt so bad they left a little note and a gift card to Starbucks, which was sweet, but Dave was still pretty annoyed because it was a vintage French press he got at a flea market...\n\nWait, sorry. The break-in. Yeah. It's been going on since then. About two years and four months.\n\nDoctor: Did the break-in at your office two years and four months ago seem to trigger this feeling of being damaged and unsafe for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. I mean... that's really why I'm here. Ever since that night, I just can't shake this feeling that work isn't safe anymore. And it's weird because our office is in this building that's supposed to have top-tier security. They actually just upgraded the badge scanners last month to these new biometric ones where you have to use your fingerprint. It's supposed to be faster, but half the time it doesn't recognize my thumb because my hands get really dry in the winter. I've been using this heavy-duty lotion with shea butter that my sister got me for my birthday, but it makes my hands so greasy that I leave smudges on my keyboard. The IT guys hate it when they have to come fix my monitor because they say I get lotion everywhere. One of them, Chris, he always brings these little alcohol wipes with him now whenever he comes to my desk...\n\nSorry, I'm doing it again. But yeah, the break-in... it just made me feel like I'm somehow damaged now. Like, before that happened, I was doing so well. I had just gotten promoted, I was handling all these big deployments, and I felt so competent. But now... it's like that person isn't really there anymore. I just feel broken.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the break-in at your office, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it happens a lot. Like, if I hear a badge scanner beeping, or heavy footsteps out in the hallway, or especially if I get a late-night deployment alert... Oh, speaking of those alerts, we actually just switched our on-call system to PagerDuty a few months ago, and they changed the default notification sound. It used to be this sort of gentle chime, but now it's this awful, jarring siren noise. My roommate absolutely hates it. She works as a pediatric nurse, so she does these exhausting 12-hour shifts, and whenever my phone goes off at 3 AM, it wakes her up too. She actually bought me this special vibrating alarm watch for Christmas so it wouldn't wake her, but the band gave me this weird rash on my wrist. I think I might be allergic to the silicone they use? I had to go to the dermatologist, and she gave me this prescription cream, but my insurance didn't want to cover it at first because they said it was cosmetic... \n\nWait, sorry, I'm drifting again. You asked if I relive it. Yeah. Whenever those things happen—the footsteps, the scanner, the alerts—it suddenly puts me right back in that office. My chest gets incredibly tight, and the image of the guard on the floor just... it just cuts through whatever I’m doing. It feels exactly like I'm back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see vivid images or replays of the break-in in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. The images are really clear. Like I was just saying, if I hear a badge scanner beep, or if someone is walking with heavy footsteps down the hall... or those late-night deployment alerts on my phone. Oh, actually, speaking of my phone, I just had to get a new one last week because I dropped my old one in a puddle outside the grocery store. It was raining so hard, and I was trying to balance my bags and my keys, and it just slipped right out of my hand. The screen completely shattered, and water got inside. I took it to one of those repair kiosks in the mall, the ones right next to the Auntie Anne's pretzels? The guy there said it was completely fried. Which was so annoying because I had all these photos from my friend Sarah's wedding on there that I hadn't backed up to the cloud yet. Sarah was furious, honestly, because she hired this photographer who ended up being terrible, so everyone was relying on cell phone pictures...\n\nBut um, sorry, what were you asking? The images. Right. Yeah, when I hear those triggers, it's like a replay in my head. I get this really tight feeling in my chest, and then I just see the security guard lying on the floor. It just cuts right through whatever else I'm doing or thinking about, and I'm just... stuck seeing it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
